ML Workflow

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why is seeing the whole map necessary before walking any single path?

The D·A·M taxonomy identifies the fundamental pillars of every ML system—Data, Algorithm, and Machine—while deployment location establishes the physical constraints each component must satisfy. Engineering organizations frequently treat these three axes as independent specialties: one team curates datasets, another tunes neural architectures, and a third provisions serving clusters. Yet the central principle of systems design is that these components remain inextricably coupled. The volume and quality of collected data bound which algorithms are viable. The computational structure of the chosen algorithm dictates what hardware can execute it efficiently. The physical memory and bandwidth of the target hardware reshape what preprocessing transformations can run at inference. Pull on any single thread and the entire equilibrium shifts.

Optimizing each component in isolation invariably produces accurate models that cannot be deployed and efficient data pipelines that serve the wrong distributions. The engineering cost of an architectural mismatch compounds with how late in the project it surfaces. A physical memory limit discovered during initial design changes a single line in a hardware specification; that same limit discovered on the eve of deployment discards months of expensive training runs, manual annotation budgets, and software integration work. A data engineer who understands how preprocessing transforms constrain downstream inference builds different pipelines than one who views curation as an isolated task. Likewise, a model architect who designs against a concrete on-device memory budget from day one avoids the trap of optimizing benchmark accuracy in an operational vacuum. Before analyzing any individual layer in isolation, engineers must master the full system map: how an ML system is specified, trained, validated, and sustained as an integrated operational loop. The ML workflow sets that map in motion through continuous co-design across data, algorithm, and machine until their emergent capability meets real-world requirements.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain the six ML lifecycle stages as coordinated data-algorithm-machine decisions with feedback
  • Compare ML workflows with traditional software using drift, nondeterminism, and operational feedback
  • Analyze how problem-definition constraints propagate through data, modeling, validation, and deployment
  • Trace late-discovery rework through dependent data, model, validation, and deployment artifacts
  • Evaluate accuracy, efficiency, reproducibility, and deployment readiness trade-offs across lifecycle stages
  • Design feedback loops that connect monitoring signals to retraining, maintenance, and revised requirements

ML Lifecycle

A typical deployment failure illustrates the danger of uncoordinated engineering. Day one: “Build a diabetic retinopathy screening model for rural clinics.” Day 90: 95 percent accuracy on the validation set used to guide development. Day 120: 96 percent accuracy after a month of architecture tuning. Day 150: the model is handed to deployment engineers. Day 151: deployment engineers report that the model needs 4 GB of memory to run. Day 152: someone checks the deployment target, a low-power edge device in each clinic whose 4–8 GB of memory is shared with the operating system, the camera pipeline, and the clinic software; the clinic’s IT plan reserves about 500 MB of it for the model. Day 153: five months of work is discarded.

The model’s accuracy was excellent. The team’s machine learning skills were excellent. The failure was a workflow failure. A deployment constraint that should have shaped every decision from day one was discovered only after the work was done. That 500 MB budget should have propagated backward to the first architecture meeting, constraining which models were even worth considering. Instead, the team optimized each component in isolation (data collection, architecture selection, training), and the integration failure appeared only when the pieces were assembled. A documented diabetic retinopathy (DR) deployment exposed analogous workflow and infrastructure gaps.1

1 Lab-to-deployment gap: Beede et al. (2020) documented this empirically for a deep-learning diabetic retinopathy screening system deployed in Thai clinics. Although the system had specialist-level accuracy in earlier validation, 393 of 1,838 submitted images (21 percent) failed its image-quality requirements, often because clinic lighting, camera maintenance, or dilation practices did not match the system’s assumptions. Those rejections added work for nurses, forced retakes or referrals, and exposed workflow and infrastructure barriers rather than simply a model-accuracy problem.

The ML workflow is an engineering framework designed to prevent integration failures by making constraints explicit at each development stage and tracing how deployment constraints propagate across Data, Algorithm, and Machine. It marks the shift from model researcher to systems engineer. A researcher optimizes individual elements: a better architecture, a cleaner dataset, a faster accelerator. A systems engineer orchestrates those elements into production systems that reliably deliver value. The day-153 failure did not stem from an isolated modeling, data, or hardware defect; it arose from the unmanaged interaction among all three. The workflow supplies the mental map that keeps technical decisions attached to the larger system.

↩︎ The deployment paradigms whose memory, latency, and power constraints the workflow propagates are established in Deployment Paradigm Framework.

2 CRISP-DM (cross-industry standard process for data mining): CRISP-DM codified data-intensive system development as six interconnected, iterative phases rather than a linear waterfall (Chapman et al. 2000); its core design principle (feedback loops between all phases) directly informs the modern ML lifecycle’s structure.

Chapman, Pete, Julian Clinton, Randy Kerber, Thomas Khabaza, Thomas Reinartz, Colin Shearer, and Rudiger Wirth. 2000. “CRISP-DM 1.0: Step-by-Step Data Mining Guide.” SPSS Inc 1: 78.
Amershi, Saleema, Andrew Begel, Christian Bird, Robert DeLine, Harald Gall, Ece Kamar, Nachiappan Nagappan, Besmira Nushi, and Thomas Zimmermann. 2019. “Software Engineering for Machine Learning: A Case Study.” 2019 IEEE/ACM 41st International Conference on Software Engineering: Software Engineering in Practice (ICSE-SEIP), 291–300. https://doi.org/10.1109/icse-seip.2019.00042.

The machine learning lifecycle is the sequence of stages this work moves through, a structured, iterative process2 that guides the development, evaluation, and improvement of ML systems (Amershi et al. 2019). The two terms divide the job. The lifecycle is what gets traversed, the stages themselves; the workflow is how the traversal is managed, the engineering discipline of orchestrating them. The formal definition of the lifecycle emphasizes continuous management rather than a one-time release.

Definition 3.1: Machine learning lifecycle

Machine learning lifecycle is the iterative engineering process of building, deploying, monitoring, and updating ML systems, where evidence from later stages can feed back to earlier stages because model performance may change after deployment.

  1. Significance: The lifecycle is a closed loop, not a linear pipeline. Statistical drift between live production traffic and the original training data serves as an early alert signal that raises the probability of accuracy loss; the exact relationship depends on the model, labels, loss, and deployment distribution. A full retraining run consumes substantial accelerator compute time (scaling with total model operations and bounded by effective hardware throughput), while acquiring fresh verified labels incurs latency and financial expense. How quickly the data drifts and how long verified labels take to arrive therefore set how often the loop must turn, which makes lifecycle maintenance a recurring operating budget as well as an engineering process.
  2. Distinction: Unlike traditional software, whose behavior changes when code, configuration, dependencies, or environment changes, ML behavior can also change as the world changes. A deployed model’s accuracy may change through distribution shift even when the code, infrastructure, and configuration remain untouched.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that the lifecycle ends at deployment. In reality, deployment is the beginning of the feedback loop: production monitoring surfaces drift, drift triggers investigation, and outcome evidence determines whether a retrained model should re-enter the deployment stage.

Managing that traversal requires systems thinking, which analyzes how a system’s parts interrelate rather than treating them in isolation. The running case study introduced below supplies a concrete system to analyze, and section 1.13 names the structural patterns that make its stages behave as one coupled system rather than a sequence of independent optimizations.

Understanding how these stages interconnect is essential before analyzing individual layers, because storage pipelines, model training, execution frameworks, and serving infrastructure each depend on decisions made in the others. Figure 1 structures ML engineering as two decoupled pipelines operating on distinct timescales. The upper data pipeline continuously transforms raw operational streams through ingestion, curation, annotation, and validation into versioned, ML-ready datasets. The lower model pipeline executes the inner development loop: training architectures, computing evaluation metrics, validating system constraints, and packaging approved artifacts for deployment. Outer-loop feedback arrows carry production telemetry and validation discoveries back to upstream data stages.

While these ten operational blocks represent the fine-grained execution tasks within an automated platform, project governance organizes around six coarser lifecycle stages (section 1.6), sequenced in the order an initiative first encounters them. First, problem definition establishes statistical objectives and physical boundaries before any pipeline executes. Second, data collection and preparation spans the entire upper pipeline to produce validated training splits. Third, model development and training executes the inner optimization loop. Fourth, evaluation and validation audits candidate checkpoints to issue a release manifest (labeled an ML certificate in figure 1). Fifth, deployment and integration installs the validated system into the serving environment. Finally, monitoring and maintenance operates the return path, routing production telemetry and observed distribution shifts back to upstream collection.

The conceptual stages of the ML lifecycle establish the architectural map of ML engineering, but abstract pipeline diagrams cannot reveal where operational friction actually binds. Translating these stages into tangible hardware, bandwidth, and clinical trade-offs requires grounding them in a concrete production system. A real-world medical diagnostic screening deployment provides that testbed.

↪ The operational tooling and infrastructure that automate these lifecycle stages at scale are developed in ML Operations.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Team A ships a diabetic retinopathy (DR) screening model and freezes all development once the model clears validation in the lab, treating subsequent tasks as standard server operations. Team B treats the launch as the beginning of an ongoing feedback loop, monitoring operational telemetry and data distributions to guide investigation and evidence-based model updates. Which team’s posture aligns with the ML lifecycle as defined in this chapter, and why?

    1. A. Team B, because the ML lifecycle is a closed loop where operational feedback, distribution drift, and real-world performance continuously reshape upstream data and model decisions.
    2. B. Team A, because once a model meets its offline validation thresholds, its statistical properties remain fixed and require only standard infrastructure maintenance.
    3. C. Team A, because changing a validated model in production introduces regulatory risk that outweighs the benefits of adapting to data drift.
    4. D. Team B, because the ML lifecycle mandates automatic daily retraining of production models regardless of whether input distributions have drifted.

  2. In the chapter’s opening failure scenario, a team spends five months developing a diagnostic model that reaches 96 percent accuracy, only to have the entire project discarded on day 153. Explain the root cause of this failure from a workflow perspective and state the systems engineering rule that would have prevented it.

  3. In the 2016 CrowdFlower data scientist survey cited in the text, respondents indicated that data-related tasks dominated their time, with 60 percent selecting ____ and organizing data as their largest time sink, compared to only 4 percent for refining algorithms.

  5. Which interpretation of the Constraint Propagation Principle is consistent with the chapter’s treatment of discovering constraints late in the ML lifecycle?

    1. A. It uses an illustrative sensitivity model in which correction cost grows roughly like \(2^{i-1}\) when a constraint is discovered at stage \(i\), with actual rework depending on which downstream artifacts were shaped by the missed constraint.
    2. B. It establishes a universal linear rule: each later lifecycle stage adds the same fixed amount of rework regardless of what artifacts were affected.
    3. C. It claims late-discovered constraints always force a complete rebuild of every downstream artifact, so the exact dependency structure does not matter.
    4. D. It implies correction cost generally falls in later stages because downstream telemetry makes fixes easier than catching the issue early.

Case Study: DR Screening

Diabetic retinopathy (DR) is a leading cause of preventable blindness worldwide.3 While early detection prevents severe vision loss, limited access to specialist ophthalmologists in rural regions motivates automated screening programs. Detecting the pathology, however, requires identifying subtle microvascular features under variable capture conditions. Figure 2 contrasts a normal retina (Panel A) against one exhibiting diabetic retinopathy (Panel B), where detecting localized lesions (such as the indicated hemorrhage) requires preserving fine spatial resolution throughout the vision pipeline and enforcing strict capture-quality validation.

3 Diabetic retinopathy (DR): DR is a common complication of diabetes; early detection can prevent vision loss, but specialist access varies substantially across countries. This gap motivates scalable screening programs. In clinics with limited hardware or unreliable connectivity, model size, offline capability, latency, and workflow integration can become binding deployment constraints.

Diabetic retinopathy screening serves as the running case study throughout this chapter because it satisfies three essential criteria: it appears straightforward on the surface but reveals deep operational friction in practice, it exercises the full deployment spectrum from cloud training to edge inference, and its systems challenges are documented across real-world deployments. The literature spans both sides of the lab-to-field divide: Gulshan et al. (2016) documented expert-level benchmark validation in controlled settings, while Beede et al. (2020) documented the severe workflow, image-quality, and infrastructure failures that emerged when deploying a deep-learning screening system in Thai clinics. Translating laboratory models into clinical deployment exposes severe operational friction: sensor lighting variations, intermittent network connectivity in rural health posts, regulatory compliance, and clinic workflow integration.4

4 Healthcare AI deployment gap: In the Thai DR deployment studied by Beede et al. (2020), real clinics exposed workflow, image-quality, infrastructure, and human-factors constraints that were absent from controlled evaluation. The case shows why laboratory metrics alone do not establish deployment readiness.

Beede, Emma, Elizabeth Baylor, Fred Hersch, Anna Iurchenko, Lauren Wilcox, Paisan Ruamviboonsuk, and Laura M. Vardoulakis. 2020. “A Human-Centered Evaluation of a Deep Learning System Deployed in Clinics for the Detection of Diabetic Retinopathy.” Proceedings of the CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems (CHI), 1–12. https://doi.org/10.1145/3313831.3376718.

To ground each lifecycle stage in physical realities, this chapter defines a concrete composite scenario: a network of rural clinics, each executing local inference on an Orin Nano-class edge device with 4 to 8 GB of shared memory and an intermittent wide-area uplink of roughly 2 Mbps. The clinical mandate requires more than 90 percent sensitivity, with an inference latency budget under 50 ms within a 100 ms end-to-end examination window. Sensitivity measures the fraction of true disease cases correctly flagged; its denominator includes only patients with the condition, meaning high aggregate accuracy on an imbalanced, mostly healthy population provides no assurance that sick patients will be detected. Specificity measures the fraction of healthy patients correctly left unflagged, bounding unnecessary referrals that would overwhelm regional specialists. Hardware limits, operator variance, and data distributions govern real-world viability far more than benchmark scores do.

Quantifying the ML Lifecycle

Practitioner time allocation exposes where the lifecycle actually bottlenecks, and engineering effort rarely concentrates where research literature spends its pages. Conceptual models of the ML lifecycle describe which stages exist, but systems decisions turn on quantitative reality: where engineering hours are spent, how much compute is consumed, and which pipeline stalls delay deployment.

Practitioner surveys reveal a stark asymmetry between where research attention centers and where engineering hours are spent, with about 79 percent of data scientists in CrowdFlower’s 2016 survey naming data collection or cleaning as the activity that consumed most of their time. Figure 3 charts the reported distribution of primary engineering time sinks, contrasting the dominant data cleaning (60 percent) and collection (19 percent) slices against the narrow share occupied by algorithm refinement (4 percent) and training set construction (3 percent).

The survey responses identify data work as a major time sink for these practitioners, without measuring how all engineering hours were divided. In a project whose collection or preparation pipeline delays useful experiments, improving that infrastructure can reduce iteration time before model or training optimizations become the binding concern. The workflow must identify its actual bottleneck rather than assign investment priorities from response shares alone.

↪ The data infrastructure that resolves these pipeline bottlenecks is developed in Data Engineering.

Beyond time allocation, iteration velocity governs project progress. The feedback arrows in figure 1 (the Data fixes loop from curation back to collection, the Data needs loop from validation back to curation, and the Online Performance path from production deployment back to collection) are not rare exceptions; they are paths teams traverse repeatedly. When data quality degrades through distribution shift or incorrect labels, rework sends teams back to collection. When an architecture exceeds accelerator memory or suffers numerical instability, engineers must revisit model design. When the serving layer exceeds latency budgets, the deployment pipeline stalls. Each loop consumes calendar time and compute budget, increasing development cost whenever downstream stages fail to catch violations early.

Over the same six months, the hourly cycle tests about four times as many ideas.

Napkin Math 3.1: The iteration tax
Problem: Compare what six months of development buys for a rural diabetic retinopathy screening system: a large ensemble developed on cloud hardware as a laboratory baseline, which would itself need compression before it could fit the clinic’s memory budget (training time: 1 week, initial accuracy: 95 percent), or a lightweight edge model (training time: 1 hour, initial accuracy: 90 percent). How do the two compare in final accuracy and in the number of hypotheses the team can test?

Math: In six months (~26 weeks), the possible experiment count is:

  1. Large model: 26 weeks of calendar time at 1 week per experiment. Each experiment improves accuracy by an assumed constant ~0.15 percentage points.
  2. Small model: 26 weeks of calendar time at 168 h/week gives 4,368 possible experiments at 1 hour each. Machine time is not the binding constraint at that cycle length, so this scenario counts only 100 of them as effective experiments, a few per working week, which is the pace at which a team can design, run, and learn from experiments. The remaining capacity sits idle for want of hypotheses. Because the slow-cycle team runs one experiment per week, this cap, not the hardware, sets the breadth ratio below; a team that could formulate useful hypotheses faster would raise the ratio in proportion. Even with a smaller assumed gain per iteration, more useful iterations can produce a larger cumulative gain.

Result: If each iteration improves accuracy by 0.1 percentage points on average, the small model starts at 90 percent and reaches 100 percent after 100 effective iterations, before applying the 99 percent ceiling. With the 99 percent ceiling applied, the small model achieves 99 percent. The large model starts at 95 percent and reaches 98.9 percent after 26 slower iterations. Both approaches end near the ceiling, and the accuracy gap between them is an artifact of the assumed gains, not a finding. The difference that survives is breadth. Under the stated cap, the fast-cycle team tested 3.8× as many ideas, so it also learned which augmentations and preprocessing choices matter, knowledge the slow-cycle team never acquired.

Systems insight: Iteration velocity is a feature. Its value lies in search breadth rather than in a guaranteed accuracy lead. When candidate systems have comparable attainable quality, shorter cycles expand how much of the design space a team can test. Speed alone cannot guarantee that a smaller model will outperform a larger one. In the DR screening scenario, the lightweight model’s rapid iteration cycle enables the team to experiment with label-preserving input transformations, preprocessing pipelines, and architecture variations far more quickly.

This cumulative delay defines the iteration tax: time spent waiting on slow training runs or cumbersome validation pipelines reduces how many candidate models a team can test. Late discoveries amplify this penalty,5 as formalized by the constraint propagation principle (section 1.5). Violations caught only after deployment invalidate artifacts produced by earlier stages, forcing expensive retraining, pipeline adjustments, and renewed validation. Intercepting defects before they cascade into systemic rework requires explicit stage interface contracts that validate outputs at every stage boundary (section 1.6.1).

5 Late correction costs: Boehm’s Software Engineering Economics (Boehm 1981) discussed how defects found late can cost more to fix than those caught during requirements. In ML systems, late-discovered constraints may require retraining, data-pipeline changes, and renewed validation.

Boehm, Barry W. 1981. Software Engineering Economics. Prentice-Hall.

The operational friction of slow iteration and cascading rework exposes a broader structural reality: ML workflows are not slow versions of traditional software lifecycles. They differ fundamentally in where engineering effort concentrates, how feedback loops reshape artifacts over time, and how silent data shifts alter system behavior.

ML vs. Traditional Software

Traditional and ML systems both iterate.6 The difference that forces new workflow controls is narrower than iteration and sharper. Rule-based software encodes explicit logic, so its behavior changes when someone changes the code, configuration, or dependencies. An ML system learns a statistical mapping from data, so its behavior can also change when the data changes while the code stays fixed. Every workflow addition this chapter introduces (versioned datasets, statistical evaluation gates, drift monitoring) exists to manage that second path.

6 Waterfall model: A plan-driven lifecycle is often depicted as a sequence of requirements, implementation, and testing, but Royce (1970) warned that a purely sequential implementation was risky and described iteration between successive phases. ML systems add data and model feedback that must be managed explicitly.

Royce, Winston W. 1970. “Managing the Development of Large Software Systems.” Proceedings of IEEE WESCON 26: 328–88.

↩︎ Data-defined behavior, and the silent degradation it makes possible, are established in ML vs. Traditional Software.

Fraud detection shows the difference in practice. A rule such as “decline if the amount exceeds $5,000 abroad” changes only when someone edits it, and a test against its specification shows what it will do. A learned fraud score changes whenever it is retrained on new transactions, and the labels that grade it (confirmed fraud cases) may arrive weeks late. Both approaches need revalidation as fraud patterns shift, and both trade lost revenue against fraud losses when setting decision thresholds. Only the learned score, however, can change its decision logic without a code change, and only fresh labeled data can show whether today’s model still catches today’s fraud. That is why its workflow must version data, gate releases on statistical evidence, and monitor for drift.

These differences alter how lifecycle stages interact. While conventional software engineering relies on production feedback to refine code and requirements, ML systems introduce feedback loops where operational data reshapes training distributions, detected drift prompts investigation, and runtime errors expose dataset blind spots. Table 1 contrasts traditional software and ML engineering across six core lifecycle dimensions, highlighting how data evolution redefines testing, deployment, and ongoing maintenance.7

7 Data versioning: Unlike code, which changes through discrete, auditable commits, data can drift gradually (distribution shift), suddenly (schema migration), or subtly (label quality degradation). Plain Git is impractical for many multi-terabyte datasets, so teams commonly version external-data manifests or pointers using tools such as DVC and Git LFS. Without data versioning, teams cannot reliably reproduce a prior training run or determine whether an accuracy regression stems from a code change or a data change.

Checkpoint 3.1: ML vs. traditional software

ML systems are not traditional software with a model attached. Check the differences that force a separate workflow:

Constraint Propagation Principle

If each later stage doubles the fix, a constraint found at monitoring costs 32× one caught at definition.

In the DR scenario, the intermittent 2 Mbps clinic uplink rules out cloud inference and requires edge deployment. The edge target’s memory and power budgets then constrain model architecture, favoring compact MobileNet-class models over large ensembles and requiring automated image-quality checks at capture time. Each downstream constraint narrows the design space for upstream stages, coupling hardware limits directly to data collection and model design. Stating that coupling precisely uses the stage numbers given alongside figure 1, from (1) problem definition to (6) monitoring and maintenance.

Definition 3.2: The constraint propagation principle

The constraint propagation principle states that a constraint discovered late in the ML lifecycle can force rework across affected upstream stages. Datasets, trained weights, validation evidence, and deployment packages record decisions that later work depends on. Correcting a missing constraint requires tracing those dependencies to determine which artifacts must change and which remain valid. Estimating actual rework requires the effort to revise each affected artifact, repeat its dependent work, and validate the resulting system.

  1. Significance: A 100 ms latency target discovered at deployment (stage 5) may propagate backward to constrain model size (stage 3: operation count \(\Operations\)), dataset requirements (stage 2: dataset size \(\DatasetSize\), the number of training examples), and problem definition (stage 1: what accuracy is achievable). Rework depends on which earlier decisions relied on the missing constraint. Within the iron law, a latency budget bounds total time \(\Time\), and therefore every term that sums to it, so it redefines the feasible region for \(\Operations\), for the bytes moved \(\DataVolume\) (distinct from the example count \(\DatasetSize\)), and for the hardware efficiency \(\EfficiencyHw\) the model must achieve.
  2. Distinction: Unlike modular decomposition (which encourages independent optimization of each component), this principle mandates end-to-end reasoning: optimizing accuracy in isolation may produce a model that is infeasible to deploy, so an accuracy optimum reached in isolation can be an infeasible system.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that deployment is “the last step.” The deployment environment is the day-one constraint: its latency budget, memory capacity, and power envelope define the boundaries of every upstream decision.

Propagation runs in both directions, so the stages form a network of constraints rather than a chain. Backward propagation carries a deployment limit upstream. When rural clinic deployment reveals tight bandwidth, the team can compress images before sending them over the uplink, but lossy compression degrades input quality, so the training dataset must then include matching compression artifacts or the model will see different inputs in service than in training (training-serving skew). Shifting inference entirely to the edge and transmitting only the diagnostic result relieves the uplink instead, but tightens the edge device’s memory capacity and power budget. Forward propagation carries an early data decision downstream. Capturing fundus images at the resolution needed to see small hemorrhages fixes the model’s input size, and with it the operation count every inference must pay on the clinic device. Upstream data choices and downstream machine boundaries remain physically coupled.

Decisions made without accounting for downstream execution boundaries accumulate technical debt.8 The stage interface specification (table 3) operationalizes this principle by making constraints explicit at each stage boundary. Those contracts enable earlier detection of constraints. When propagation occurs specifically through data quality failures, the resulting pattern is known as a data cascade: a chain of downstream failures triggered by bad data (Sambasivan et al. 2021).

8 ML technical debt: Sculley et al. (2015) identify ML-specific debt mechanisms such as entanglement (changing one feature affects all others because the model learned joint distributions), hidden feedback loops (predictions influence future training data), and undeclared consumers (downstream systems depending on outputs without contracts). Since ML code is often only a small part of a production system, the surrounding configuration, pipelines, and infrastructure can allow this debt to accumulate silently.

Sculley, D., Gary Holt, Daniel Golovin, Eugene Davydov, Todd Phillips, Dietmar Ebner, Vinay Chaudhary, Michael Young, Jean-François Crespo, and Dan Dennison. 2015. “Hidden Technical Debt in Machine Learning Systems.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 28: 2503–11.
Sambasivan, Nithya, Shivani Kapania, Hannah Highfill, Diana Akrong, Praveen Paritosh, and Lora M Aroyo. 2021. “‘Everyone Wants to Do the Model Work, Not the Data Work’: Data Cascades in High-Stakes AI.” Proceedings of the 2021 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems, 1–15. https://doi.org/10.1145/3411764.3445518.

↪ Shared contracts and deployment paths that reject incompatible changes before they reach production are enforced in Development Infrastructure.

Lifecycle Stages

The rural-clinic failure illustrates why ML projects require an explicit six-stage framework: deployment constraints surfaced only after data curation, model training, and evaluation decisions had hardened around an unfeasible target. Preventing such misalignments requires establishing clear ownership across the system stack.

These six stages delineate project responsibilities, mapping directly onto the D·A·M axes. Problem definition sets the governing requirements for all three. Data collection and preparation owns the Data axis, model development and training governs the Algorithm axis, and deployment and integration commits both to a physical Machine. Evaluation and monitoring own no single axis of their own; their role is verification, testing whether the three axes function harmoniously prior to release and continuously in production. Figure 4 arranges these stages as a closed operational loop: problem definition flows into data preparation, model development, and validation across the top row, while deployment transitions into monitoring and maintenance below, routing telemetry back to upstream collection.

The DR team’s choice of model shows the stages acting on one decision. Problem definition fixed the clinic device’s 500 MB model budget, so model development starts from architectures designed for budgets of that kind. MobileNetV2, a model built for real-time inference on mobile-class hardware, is a natural candidate backbone, at about 14 MB of FP32 weights and about 600 MFLOP per inference. It reaches that operation count by replacing standard image-processing layers with a cheaper variant9 rather than by pruning a larger model. Choosing it immediately constrains the other stages. Data preparation must produce images at the input resolution the backbone expects, evaluation must measure latency on the clinic device as well as sensitivity, and deployment must confirm that weights, activations, and runtime together fit the budget. If on-device evaluation then misses the latency target, the fix loops back to architecture or input resolution, so the stage sequence in the diagram is traversed as a cycle rather than once.

9 Depthwise separable convolutions: The layer type MobileNetV2 uses in place of standard convolutions. It splits one expensive operation into two cheaper ones and cuts computation by roughly 8–9\(\times\) for typical kernel sizes (Sandler et al. 2018), which is what makes the roughly 600 MFLOP inference budget plausible on mobile-class hardware.

Sandler, Mark, Andrew Howard, Menglong Zhu, Andrey Zhmoginov, and Liang-Chieh Chen. 2018. “MobileNetV2: Inverted Residuals and Linear Bottlenecks.” 2018 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 4510–20. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2018.00474.
Checkpoint 3.2: The workflow cycle

Verify that each stage enforces contracts that bind upstream choices to physical deployment constraints.

Beyond procedural orchestration, each lifecycle stage acts on terms of the iron law of ML systems, and because the binding constraint differs across workload archetypes, each stage optimizes different terms for different workloads. ResNet-50, DLRM, and keyword spotting (KWS) are useful anchors because each stresses a different part of the system: dense vision training tries to keep accelerators busy, sparse recommendation spends much of its time moving embedding rows, and keyword spotting is constrained first by tiny memory and always-on energy budgets.

↩︎ The iron law of ML systems and its governing terms are established in Iron Law of ML Systems.

Production systems rarely fall neatly into a single archetype. A medical imaging classifier, for instance, requires sustained computation while training over large image datasets, yet faces strict energy and memory limits when deployed to portable clinic hardware. Understanding how the workflow framework adapts across these trade-offs is essential for sound engineering decisions. Table 2 follows three of the six stages (abbreviated in its rows as data collection and preparation, model development and training, and deployment and integration) across these recurring workload archetypes. Its DLRM row uses p99 latency, the time within which 99 percent of requests complete, because users of an interactive service notice its slow tail, not its average.

Each stage of this workflow presents distinct systems challenges, from curating multi-terabyte datasets to sustaining tail latency in production. Grounding these challenges requires a quantitative lens on how stages govern execution.

Systems Perspective 3.1: The iron law of workflow
The six lifecycle stages alter distinct terms in the iron law of ML systems \((\Time = \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} + \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw} + \Latency)\). Rather than rigid one-to-one assignments, the mappings serve as diagnostic starting points:

  • Problem definition: Sets target constraints for accuracy, latency, cost, privacy, and deployment paradigm (screened across privacy, latency, compute, and cost gates using the decision framework in Deployment Decision Screening). These targets determine which terms of the equation are permitted to grow and which must be bounded from the outset.
  • Data collection and preparation: Shapes dataset composition and the byte volume \((\DataVolume)\) presented to downstream pipelines. Curation can also change the training work required to reach a quality target.
  • Model development and training: Dictates the operation count \((\Operations)\), data reuse patterns, parameter movement, and achievable hardware efficiency \((\EfficiencyHw)\).
  • Evaluation and validation: Tests whether model quality and end-to-end execution meet deployment requirements on the target system.
  • Deployment and integration: Determines the serving path, including runtime data movement, execution efficiency, and fixed overhead \((\Latency)\).
  • Monitoring and maintenance: Observes distribution drift, latency, throughput, and operational cost after launch, feeding violations back into earlier stages for re-optimization.

Viewing the lifecycle through the iron law grounds latency and cost in hardware physics. Production workflows must simultaneously satisfy accuracy, safety, privacy, and organizational constraints that sit outside the equation.

Stage interface specification

Each lifecycle stage operates as a distinct engineering phase with defined inputs, outputs, and quality invariants. In production, pipeline orchestrators encode these dependencies as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs), where nodes represent discrete execution tasks and directed edges represent validated artifacts. While the overarching ML lifecycle forms a recurring feedback loop over weeks and months, each individual pipeline run executes as a deterministic, acyclic sequence of dependent steps. This structure enables the orchestrator to parallelize independent branches, cache validated intermediate outputs, and halt execution the moment a contract is violated. Just as microservice architectures rely on interface specifications to enforce API boundaries, ML pipelines require strict schema, distribution, and performance contracts to prevent defective data or degraded weights from corrupting downstream artifacts. Table 3 formalizes these contracts, defining the inputs, deliverables, and quality invariants across all six stages.

Enforcing explicit contracts transforms the conceptual feedback loop in figure 4 into an operational auditing mechanism. When validation or monitoring surfaces a defect, the contract boundary identifies which upstream stage owns the remedy. For example, a mismatch between training features and production inputs may demand targeted data collection, whereas an error in feature transformation points directly to a bug in the serving pipeline. Intercepting these violations at stage boundaries prevents defective assumptions from hardening into late-stage architectural rework, as demonstrated in the transition audit below.

Example 3.1: Auditing stage transitions
Scenario: An engineering team claims to have completed Problem Definition for a medical imaging classifier, attempting to transition to Data Collection without establishing deployment target constraints.

Diagnosis: The output contract lacks deployment paradigm selection and resource constraints (latency and memory targets). If the violation surfaces only at deployment as a trained model larger than the edge device’s 500 MB model budget, the audit must trace what relied on the missing limit. A smaller candidate may require revised input-resolution or preprocessing choices, new weights, repeated sensitivity and on-device memory tests, and a replacement deployment package. Existing raw images may remain usable; changing the candidate does not imply recollecting every image.

Repair schedule: Suppose the repair plan preserves the raw images and labels but changes input preparation and the candidate architecture. Assume 1 day to revise preparation, 2 days to train new weights, 2 days to repeat validation, and 1 day to package the approved result. If each task waits for the preceding artifact and the required staff and compute are available, the repair takes 6 days. This is a supplied schedule for this dependency chain, not a multiplier determined by the discovery stage. Queueing, failed validation, or parallel independent tests would change the elapsed time.

Systems lesson: Stage transitions act as quality gates in the ML lifecycle. Enforcing strict output contract validation before proceeding downstream prevents expensive upstream rework and ensures data collection aligns with target deployment hardware.

Without explicit contract gates, unstated assumptions propagate downstream. The earliest opportunity to bound them is Problem Definition, which records the operational constraints that subsequent stages must satisfy.

Self-Check: Question

  1.   2. An engineering team completes Problem Definition with clinical sensitivity targets but marks the target deployment paradigm as ‘TBD — to be determined after model training.’ According to the Stage Interface Specification, what should the transition audit verdict be, and why?

    1. A. Approved, because decoupling model development from hardware targets allows researchers to maximize accuracy before applying post-hoc pruning.
    2. B. Approved with warning, provided the team commits to using cloud inference if the model exceeds edge memory budgets.
    3. C. Blocked, because Problem Definition’s output contract explicitly requires deployment paradigm and resource constraints to be established before data collection and modeling begin.
    4. D. Blocked only if the model architecture requires distributed multi-GPU training, since single-device models can adapt to any deployment target.

  3. The chapter considers MobileNetV2 (about 14 MB of FP32 weights and ~600 MFLOPs per inference) as a candidate backbone for the DR clinic device, whose model budget was fixed at Problem Definition. Explain how choosing a model to fit that budget propagates across Data Collection, Model Development, and Evaluation.

  4. Which mapping between lifecycle stages and the terms in the Iron Law of ML Systems (\(T = \frac{D_{\text{vol}}}{\text{BW}} + \frac{O}{R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}} + L_{\text{lat}}\)) is conceptually consistent with the chapter?

    1. A. Problem Definition exclusively determines \(R_{\text{peak}}\), and later lifecycle stages cannot materially affect hardware throughput or efficiency once the use case is chosen.
    2. B. Model Development and Training exclusively determines \(O\), while Data Collection and Preparation cannot affect bytes moved and Deployment and Integration cannot affect latency overhead.
    3. C. Deployment and Integration exclusively determines \(L_{\text{lat}}\), while earlier stages cannot materially affect operation count, data volume, or serving-path overhead once deployment begins.
    4. D. Data Collection and Preparation is diagnostically associated with bytes moved such as \(D_{\text{vol}}\); Model Development and Training is associated with computational work \(O\) and opportunities for reuse; Deployment and Integration is associated with serving-path overhead such as \(L_{\text{lat}}\), even though these influences are not exclusive.

Problem Definition

Problem definition in ML begins with sentences that look deceptively simple. A product manager writes: “Build a model that detects diabetic retinopathy.” That single sentence conceals a dozen engineering decisions: sensitivity thresholds for patient safety, hardware capabilities in rural clinics, latency budgets that keep clinicians engaged, and regulatory frameworks governing approval. Software requirements still govern control flow, interfaces, and data storage. ML adds an empirical obligation: defining what the system should do is inseparable from defining how it will learn from data and what physical constraints its execution must survive.

The request for a model must first become a requirement for a useful improvement. In the clinic case, the baseline is the existing screening and referral workflow, evaluated on the outcomes and resource limits the proposed system must improve. A simpler rule or an existing model can provide an additional comparison where appropriate. If available data cannot support the required evaluation, or the expected benefit does not justify the added errors, cost, and maintenance, the team should revise the problem or retain the baseline rather than proceed automatically to training.

Balancing these demands requires reasoning across the full system stack. An engineering team cannot optimize accuracy in isolation: pursuing marginal sensitivity gains by scaling model depth or adopting a larger model family inflates parameter count and computational demands (FLOPs per inference), directly challenging the memory capacity and thermal limits of low-cost clinic hardware. Conversely, pruning a model to fit an embedded accelerator’s DRAM budget risks dropping sensitivity below clinical safety thresholds. Every technical decision operates within a multi-constraint optimization problem where statistical goals, physical execution budgets, and operational mandates continually bound each other’s feasible design space.

Constraint layers

Every ML problem definition decomposes into three interacting constraint layers: statistical, physical, and operational. Rather than isolated requirements, these layers form a coupled stack where constraints in one tier immediately restrict the degrees of freedom in the others. The statistical layer specifies target behavior over data distributions, such as achieving \(>90\) percent sensitivity and \(>80\) percent specificity across diverse patient demographics and camera models, rather than an unweighted aggregate accuracy that conceals failure modes on minority subpopulations. The physical layer bounds execution within hardware limits: for rural clinics, inferencing on local edge accelerators under tight thermal budgets, bounded memory capacity (such as 4 to 8 GB of shared DRAM), and a 100 ms end-to-end latency budget to keep clinical workflows interactive without depending on intermittent network uplinks. The operational layer encompasses clinical auditability, regulatory clearance, and governance frameworks that dictate long-term system maintenance.

The operational layer carries physical consequences that traditional software engineering rarely encounters. In a traditional database, deleting a patient’s record is a DELETE statement. In an ML system, model weights may encode statistical patterns learned from that record: where law or a regulator requires removing that influence, compliance may require machine unlearning (an active research area with incomplete guarantees) or retraining on the remaining data. The obligation and remedy depend on the governing rule and system; at DR-system scale, either can make privacy compliance a recurring compute-budget item and training provenance an architectural constraint.

The constraint propagation principle (section 1.5) explains why all three layers must be bound before development begins: a constraint that exists in the physical or operational environment but remains unstated in the problem definition does not vanish. It re-emerges later as an architectural violation after dependent decisions have hardened. For example, if an engineering team defines the objective solely as aggregate classification accuracy on retinal images, standard optimization will minimize the average training loss, which the majority class dominates. In an imbalanced screening population where 90 percent of patients are healthy, a model predicting “no disease” achieves 90 percent aggregate accuracy while failing every patient who needs urgent care. Preventing this requires translating a domain invariant (a missed diagnosis costs far more than a false referral) into an explicit statistical contract: class-weighted loss, a sensitivity floor, and subgroup targets.

War Story 3.1: When the label was the bias (2018)
Context: In 2014, Amazon assembled an engineering team at its Edinburgh hub to build an internal machine-learning system for ranking technical job applicants, training it on ten years of resumes submitted to Amazon (Dastin 2018).

Mechanism: Because prior hiring was male-dominated, the model learned that male-coded language correlated with hiring success, systematically penalizing terms like “women’s” and downgrading graduates of all-women’s colleges.

Impact: The model reproduced historical hiring bias, rendering automated resume screening unsuitable for production recruitment.

Fix: Amazon abandoned the experimental tool after attempts to remove explicit gender signals could not guarantee that it would not learn other discriminatory proxies.

Systems lesson: Problem definition must specify fairness, auditability, and rejection criteria before data collection and training. A workflow trained on biased labels does not learn the operational goal; it learns to reproduce the labels.

Dastin, Jeffrey. 2018. “Amazon Scraps Secret AI Recruiting Tool That Showed Bias Against Women.” Reuters.

Problem definitions evolve

A problem definition is not a static specification frozen at project inception; it evolves as deployment expands. When a DR screening program scales from a pilot clinic with a calibrated fundus camera to regional health centers with heterogeneous imaging hardware and differing patient demographics,10 the initial assumptions break. Sensor noise profiles diverge, optical resolutions shift, and demographic variations alter subgroup error rates. Adapting to this operational reality forces the problem definition to incorporate stratified accuracy targets and broader hardware compatibility matrices.

10 Demographic drift: Population changes can expose performance gaps that aggregate evaluation obscures. Facial-analysis benchmarks found large demographic error-rate disparities (Buolamwini and Gebru 2018), while a healthcare risk-scoring study found racially biased allocation from a proxy label despite similar need (Obermeyer et al. 2019). These examples illustrate why a growing DR program should evaluate subgroup performance rather than claiming that such a rollout occurred.

Buolamwini, Joy, and Timnit Gebru. 2018. “Gender Shades: Intersectional Accuracy Disparities in Commercial Gender Classification.” Conference on Fairness, Accountability and Transparency, 77–91.
Obermeyer, Ziad, Brian Powers, Christine Vogeli, and Sendhil Mullainathan. 2019. “Dissecting Racial Bias in an Algorithm Used to Manage the Health of Populations.” Science 366 (6464): 447–53. https://doi.org/10.1126/science.aax2342.

This dynamic evolution stems directly from the statistical nature of ML. Scaling exposes long-tail inputs absent from pilot evaluations, while production environments shift the input distribution in ways no static offline dataset fully captures. A robust problem definition anticipates this drift by establishing governance protocols upfront: defining which monitoring signals trigger formal re-evaluation and bounding how threshold updates propagate through subsequent pipeline stages. Once these operational constraints and performance boundaries are defined on paper, the workflow must ground them in physical artifacts, beginning with the data collection pipelines that gather the training distribution.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does the statement ‘Build a computer vision model that detects diabetic retinopathy’ fail as a complete problem definition for an ML system?

    1. A. It specifies only a high-level task while omitting the statistical constraint layers (sensitivity/specificity floors across subgroups), physical constraints (edge device memory/latency budgets), and operational constraints (regulatory compliance, clinical workflow integration).
    2. B. It fails to specify which exact deep neural network backbone and learning rate schedule must be used during training.
    3. C. It defines an image classification problem when medical AI systems must always be framed as unsupervised anomaly detection tasks.
    4. D. It defines quantifiable objectives before data collection has occurred, which violates standard ML agile practices.

  2. Explain why ophthalmologists and clinic administrators must participate directly in Problem Definition for a DR screening system, rather than being consulted only during clinical evaluation.

  3. In the 2018 Amazon automated recruiting war story cited in the chapter, an ML model trained on ten years of resumes was abandoned because it systematically penalized female applicants. What fundamental systems lesson does this case illustrate regarding Problem Definition?

    1. A. Resume screening models require recurrent neural networks rather than transformer architectures to avoid learning gendered proxies.
    2. B. Offline evaluation metrics are inherently incapable of measuring demographic disparities in supervised learning models.
    3. C. A model trained on historical data learns to reproduce historical label biases rather than the intended operational goal; fairness criteria and auditability must be explicitly defined at Problem Definition.
    4. D. Multi-class classification algorithms should not be applied to human evaluation tasks where ground truth is subjective.

  4. True or False: When a diabetic retinopathy screening deployment scales from a 3-clinic pilot to 200 clinics across diverse regions, the high-level clinical intent (detect referable retinopathy early) remains stable, but the specific engineering targets (subgroup sensitivity thresholds, device latency budgets, and camera-specific preprocessing rules) must evolve.

Data Collection and Preparation

Constraints defined on paper only take effect when grounded in actual data. Moving from problem definition to data collection connects abstract accuracy targets with operational reality: sensor noise, missing records, and network limits. This stage determines dataset size \((\DatasetSize)\), the number of examples, and through it the iron law’s byte volume \((\DataVolume)\), the bytes that storage systems, networks, and memory buses must move; the two grow together but are not the same quantity, since image resolution and encoding set how many bytes each example costs. The deployment constraints established during problem definition become technical data requirements: if the model must run on edge devices, the collection pipeline must produce inputs compatible with edge preprocessing; if it must achieve 90 percent sensitivity across diverse populations, the dataset must sample those subpopulations sufficiently to support model training.

Data collection and preparation is an ongoing engineering discipline rather than a one-time task. For diabetic retinopathy screening, these criteria demand a collection strategy far more stringent than standard computer vision benchmarks. The pipeline must assemble roughly \(10^5\) retinal fundus photographs across diverse patient cohorts and imaging hardware, with each image adjudicated by multiple ophthalmologists to establish consensus ground-truth labels capable of withstanding clinical and regulatory scrutiny. The annotation protocol must capture fine pathological features—including microaneurysms, hemorrhages, and hard exudates—across the entire disease severity continuum. Curation also governs computational efficiency: not all captured data contributes equally to training, and intelligent selection (The case for smaller datasets) can eliminate redundant samples while preserving the statistical coverage required for generalizable performance.

Each retinal photograph spans several megabytes, and clinical protocols capture multiple views per patient, immediately creating a physical infrastructure bottleneck. A busy rural facility generates gigabytes of raw imaging data each day—a volume that would overwhelm an intermittent 2 Mbps uplink during operating hours. Rather than saturating limited wide-area bandwidth with raw uploads, the system can execute inference locally on the edge accelerator and transmit only a compact edge summary: a structured record containing the diagnostic classification and clinical metadata. As quantified in Napkin math 3.2, local inference reduces uplink traffic from gigabytes to kilobytes per patient.

Edge summaries shrink the clinic’s daily uplink about 5,000-fold.

Napkin Math 3.2: Bandwidth vs. compute
Problem: Quantify the uplink window occupancy for raw image uploads from a busy rural clinic capturing retinal images for diabetic retinopathy screening, and evaluate architectural alternatives to relieve link contention.

Math:

  1. Daily data: At 150 patients/day, 10 photos/patient, and 5 MB/photo, the clinic produces 7.5 GB/day.
  2. Upload time: The uplink is 2 Mb/s, or 0.25 MB/s. Dividing 7,500 MB by that rate gives 30,000 s, approximately 8.3 h.
  3. Constraint: If the clinic requires same-shift upload during its 8 h operating window, this transfer alone occupies the link for 104.2 percent of that window and may contend with other traffic.

Systems insight: Local inference with uploaded detection summaries (10 KB/patient) reduces bandwidth usage by 5,000×. Overnight batching, compression, scheduling, or a faster link are alternatives not compared by this calculation.

Lab-to-field data gap

Laboratory data and production data inhabit different worlds. This lab-to-field gap appears when DR screening deploys to rural clinics across Thailand and India: images arrive from diverse camera equipment operated by staff with varying expertise, often under suboptimal lighting with inconsistent patient positioning. A model trained on high-quality research images from standardized fundus cameras may fail on blurry, poorly lit images from older equipment, not because the algorithm is wrong but because the data distribution has shifted beyond the training envelope.

Because raw image uploads can exceed clinic uplink capacity, edge deployment offers an alternative; this is the data locality invariant (2.3) applied to a clinic uplink. Deploying edge accelerators such as the NVIDIA Jetson family11 allows clinics to preprocess and run inference locally. This reduces network transmission by orders of magnitude, but introduces a hardware trade-off: use a smaller model that fits a low-power edge budget, or provision more capable on-premise devices at higher cost.

11 NVIDIA Jetson: NVIDIA’s Jetson family spans a wide SKU spectrum, from Jetson Orin Nano (7–15 W) through Jetson Orin NX (10–25 W) to Jetson AGX Orin (15–60 W). This scenario uses an Orin Nano-class device (4–8 GB shared LPDDR5, 7–15 W power envelope) as a concrete edge target. Its memory and power budgets constrain model complexity, while selecting a larger device changes both the feasible model and the per-clinic cost.

The bandwidth constraint makes infrastructure a data-collection decision rather than a late implementation detail. The scenario architecture combines edge devices for local inference and preprocessing, clinic aggregation servers for data management and buffering, and cloud training infrastructure for periodic model updates. It targets end-to-end latency under 100 milliseconds, of which model inference may take at most 50 ms, and operation without connectivity-induced delays.

Privacy constraints impose a similar architectural decision. Patient privacy regulations may motivate local retention or distributed training, but they generally specify protections and permitted uses rather than one required training topology. Workflows that keep raw clinic data local while sharing only approved updates or summaries add complexity to both data collection and model training infrastructure.

Distributed data infrastructure

If a deployment grows from a handful of clinics to hundreds, data infrastructure must scale accordingly. Each retinal image travels through multiple stages: clinic cameras capture the image, local systems provide initial storage and processing, quality validation checks ensure usability, secure transmission moves data to central systems, and finally, integration with training datasets completes the pipeline. The infrastructure decisions at each stage are shaped by the deployment constraints established during problem definition.

Storage placement is another data-collection decision that either preserves or erodes downstream iteration velocity. The DR images serve three distinct operational roles. The current training set is read in full every epoch, recent datasets and validation sets are read occasionally, and the archive kept for regulatory audit is almost never read. No single storage medium serves all three economically, so teams implement tiered storage architectures,12 each tier matched to how often and how fast its data must be read.

12 Tiered storage: Places data on different storage media based on access frequency and performance requirements. The storage price gap is roughly 4.3× in this example: Non-Volatile Memory Express (NVMe) SSDs deliver 500,000+ input/output operations per second (IOPS) for random reads at ~$0.10/GB/month, while object storage costs ~$0.023/GB/month but incurs 100–200 ms request latency. For ML training loops requiring sustained sequential streaming at 1–10 GB/s, fetching thousands of individual image files introduces random seeking and protocol overhead that collapses throughput, converting a compute-bound training loop into an I/O-bound stall that starves accelerator execution units.

The training set benefits from hot storage (fast local SSDs) when its repeated reads would otherwise arrive more slowly than the accelerator consumes them. Packing images into large files and fetching them ahead of use can also make an object-storage stream sufficient if its measured delivery rate meets that demand. Recent datasets and validation sets sit on warm object storage, which costs several times less per gigabyte but answers each request far more slowly. The audit archive sits on cold archival storage, the cheapest per gigabyte and the slowest to retrieve. The workflow decision is which tier a training run reads from. A run whose warm-storage requests leave the accelerator waiting loses the iteration velocity the iteration tax showed to be valuable, while keeping the archive on hot storage pays a premium for data no one reads. The boundary between tiers moves over time. A dataset can migrate from warm to hot when a training run needs faster delivery, and from hot to cold when its access rate falls, under automated policies keyed to training schedules and access recency.

↪ The cost and throughput of each storage tier, and why ML systems combine them, are measured in Storage performance and cost.

While tiered storage optimizes I/O within the centralized training cluster, ingesting data from hundreds of remote clinics introduces a distributed systems challenge. Rural clinic deployments face severe connectivity constraints that dictate transmission strategies. Facilities with reliable broadband can stream diagnostic summaries in near-real-time, whereas clinics with intermittent satellite links require store-and-forward architectures that buffer data locally and synchronize opportunistically during connectivity windows. This design choice propagates through the physical stack: store-and-forward clinics require expanded local storage buffers, autonomous edge inference runtimes, and reconciliation protocols to resolve divergences between local preliminary classifications and subsequent centralized audit reviews.

Infrastructure scalability presents a steeper challenge than raw storage capacity. As a deployment expands from a pilot site to hundreds of clinics, data heterogeneity scales alongside byte volume: divergent camera optics, erratic clinic illumination, and variable operator training yield distinct input distributions. The data pipeline must process increasing throughput while maintaining strict provenance metadata—recording camera model, capture settings, and operator ID for every image. This provenance trail proves essential for isolating site-specific accuracy regressions and satisfying regulatory audit requirements. Each clinic operates as a distinct data source.13

13 Distributed clinic data: Training across clinic sites without simply pooling all raw data addresses privacy and governance constraints, but it shifts cost into coordination. Each site may use different cameras, serve different patient populations, and follow different operating routines, so the system must track where data came from and how each site differs. The workflow cost is therefore not just storage capacity; it is the engineering work needed to compare, validate, and update models across sites whose data does not behave identically.

Coordination infrastructure—including shared artifact registries, versioned data contracts, and automated validation gates—makes site-level variance visible before it corrupts model training. Crucially, this distributed heterogeneity makes point-of-capture validation mandatory: discovering that a clinic’s camera has been miscalibrated weeks later during a centralized training run wastes storage, compute, and patient follow-up opportunities.

Quality assurance and validation

A blurry retinal image that slips past quality checks does not merely waste storage. If such images enter training with unreliable labels or appear frequently in production, they distort the statistical evidence available to the model and degrade clinical safety. Quality assurance ensures that incoming data satisfies the invariants downstream stages depend upon. In the diabetic retinopathy scenario, automated quality-scoring models deployed at the fundus camera immediately flag out-of-focus or improperly framed captures, prompting clinical staff to retake the photograph while the patient is still present rather than discovering the defect weeks later during model training.

↪ Data cascades, in which uncaught collection defects compound into downstream training and evaluation failures, are developed in Data cascades.

Validation extends beyond image quality to verify proper labeling, patient association, and privacy compliance. Local validation catches problems at the point of capture; centralized validation detects distributional anomalies across the full clinic network, for instance by flagging when a particular site’s images skew toward a narrow demographic range that would bias the training set.

Data collection decisions directly constrain model development: bandwidth limits dictate what architectures are feasible, privacy requirements shape training pipelines, and quality variations across clinic environments determine robustness requirements. Figure 5 traces how downstream operational discoveries route corrections back across upstream stage boundaries. The figure zooms into stages 2 through 6, drawing data collection and data preparation as separate boxes and omitting problem definition, which sits upstream of every path shown. In the diabetic retinopathy deployment, when model evaluation uncovers performance deficits on legacy fundus cameras, the Data gaps pathway triggers targeted image collection from those specific clinics. Similarly, when validation across diverse patient cohorts reveals lower sensitivity for patients with cataracts, the Validation Issues vector motivates retraining with synthetic lens-opacity augmentations. In production, runtime telemetry routes along the Data Quality Issues and Performance Insights paths back into data preparation and collection whenever clinic hardware upgrades alter optical characteristics.

These feedback pathways settle what data the training set contains. A curated, validated dataset can still starve the next stage, however, if the order in which training reads it fights the storage system that holds it.

Sample order and read locality

The DR training procedure considered here reads the training set, on the order of \(10^5\) fundus photographs, in full on every epoch (one complete pass over the training set), using a fresh random order each time. Whether the accelerator stays busy during those passes comes down to one comparison: the rate at which the data loader delivers images against the rate at which training consumes them. If delivery is slower, the accelerator idles however fast it is. The workflow must therefore record both the sample order training requires and the delivery rate the loader actually achieves, and test one against the other before treating the data handoff as complete.

This is a different locality question from the data locality invariant (2.3) applied earlier to the clinic uplink, which decides where computation runs relative to the data. Here the data is already placed, and the question is the order in which training reads it. Training shuffles examples across epochs to reduce dependence on their stored order; shuffling alone does not guarantee independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples. Randomized order works against the locality that operating systems exploit,14 the tendency for a program that reads byte \(\ReadByteAddress\) to read nearby bytes soon and to reuse recently accessed memory.

14 Locality of reference: Denning (1968) formalized the working-set principle for virtual memory. Randomly ordered examples can reduce locality when the logical order maps to scattered physical reads, but the effect depends on storage layout, batching, caching, and prefetching.

Denning, Peter J. 1968. “The Working Set Model for Program Behavior.” Communications of the ACM 11 (5): 323–33. https://doi.org/10.1145/363095.363141.

When the random sample order maps to scattered physical reads, shuffling weakens the file-system buffers that keep recently read blocks in memory and the prefetchers that fetch the next blocks before they are requested. The penalty depends on how logical examples map to physical storage. Datasets are usually packed into shards, large files that each hold many examples. Reading whole shards contiguously and shuffling within and across them retains useful locality, whereas issuing small reads scattered across many shards exposes the full latency of storage and network on every request. Layout-aware batching, caching, and explicit prefetching can restore locality and reduce these stalls. The relevant systems variable is the physical access pattern presented to the memory hierarchy, not randomization alone.

That distinction determines where to optimize when the delivery test fails. If the loader already issues large sequential reads, more caching adds little benefit. If it fans out small reads across many shards, repacking records or increasing the size of each read request matters far more than adding compute. The storage mechanisms themselves belong to data engineering; the workflow’s responsibility is to run the delivery test and route a failure to the right fix.

↩︎ I/O stalls and storage throughput bottlenecks limit pipelined training progress under the bottleneck principle established in The bottleneck principle.

Data collection therefore does not end when training begins. Feedback from evaluation and production keeps revising what is collected, and the delivery test keeps checking whether the loader can feed what the model consumes. What this stage hands forward is a versioned dataset, its preprocessing pipeline, and a measured delivery rate, which together become the raw material that model development turns into trained models.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A rural clinic captures 150 patients per day for DR screening, with 10 retinal photos per patient at 5 MB per photo. The clinic operates on an 8-hour daily shift with a 2 Mbps uplink. According to the chapter’s Bandwidth vs. Compute analysis, what operational bottleneck arises, and how does edge inference resolve it?

    1. A. Daily raw image upload requires ~2.5 hours, which fits comfortably within the 8-hour window without needing edge processing.
    2. B. Daily raw image upload generates 7.5 GB of data requiring ~8.3 hours to transfer—saturating the entire 8-hour clinic shift—whereas edge inference uploading 10 KB detection summaries reduces network traffic by roughly 5,000\(\times\).
    3. C. Daily raw image upload generates 75 GB of data, which exceeds daily satellite uplink capacity by a factor of 100\(\times\) regardless of compression.
    4. D. Raw uploads complete in 45 minutes, but cloud GPU queuing delay adds 12 hours of fixed inference latency.

  2. Explain why a DR screening model achieving an AUC of 0.99 on a curated laboratory research dataset can see its sensitivity fall well below the 90 percent screening target when deployed to rural clinics.

  3. In tiered storage architectures for ML pipelines, placing active training data in cold or warm object storage (e.g., S3 Standard with 100–200 ms latency) instead of local high-throughput NVMe SSDs directly degrades training performance by affecting which term in the Iron Law of ML Systems?

    1. A. It increases the Operations (\(O\)) term by forcing the model to compute extra gradient updates.
    2. B. It decreases peak hardware performance (\(R_{\text{peak}}\)) by downclocking GPU compute cores.
    3. C. It decreases hardware utilization efficiency (\(\eta_{\text{hw}}\)) solely through floating-point precision mismatches.

    1. A. Random shuffling makes the training workload strictly compute-bound, so the accelerator cores become overloaded by stochastic gradient calculations.
    2. B. Random sample access across multi-terabyte storage defeats operating system spatial and temporal locality, causing page cache misses and I/O latency stalls that additional compute cannot resolve.
    3. C. Shuffling multi-terabyte datasets bypasses the operating system page cache entirely, forcing floating-point arithmetic units to stall on instruction decoding.
